## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region each step
- Target region: strip with BLUE bordering walls, BLACK floor
- Non-target strip: GREEN walls, also BLACK floor
- Between strips: GRAY floor
- Goal: Get all robots into the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK (both strips), GRAY (middle) — can't distinguish target from non-target by floor alone
- Wall colors: BLUE (target side), GREEN (non-target side)
- Camera can detect colored objects (walls)

**Strategy:**
- Robots should move TOWARD blue walls (GoToColor with blue=3) and AWAY from green walls (GoAwayColor with green=2)
- When on BLACK floor (could be target or wrong strip), use wall color to confirm position
- The champion uses GoToColor(blue) in state 0, which is the right instinct

**Champion Analysis (score 18647.88):**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue), transitions on perceiving blue, black floor, gray floor, white floor
- State 1: Repulsion, transitions on neighbor counts and gray floor
- State 2: Exploration, transitions on gray floor and neighbor count

The champion already has a solid blue-seeking strategy. Let me design variants:

1. **SURVIVAL**: Copy champion exactly
2. **EXPLOITATION**: Tweak probabilities/gains of champion
3. **EXPLORATION**: New topologies focusing on blue-seeking + staying put on black floor

Key insight: Robots should:
1. Seek blue walls actively
2. When they see blue walls AND are on black floor → stay/stop
3. When on gray floor → move toward blue
4. When seeing green walls → move away

Let me design 14 machines:

Wait, I'm confused. Let me re-read more carefully. "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0." So D is an index into the list of OTHER states. From state 0 with 3 states total, others=[1,2], so D=0→state1, D=1→state2. D=2 would be invalid. But champion has n0x3 D=2... Hmm, maybe D indexes all states and just can't be the current state? I need to just trust the champion works and focus on improvements.

**Key insight I'm missing:** The floor is BLACK in BOTH strips. The robot can't distinguish target from non-target by floor color alone. It needs to use WALL COLOR (blue=target, green=non-target). GoToColor(blue) is the right main strategy.

**New ideas:**
1. Simple 2-state: GoToColor(blue) always, Stop when on black floor and seeing blue
2. GoToColor(blue) + GoAwayColor(green) combination
3. Use camera to detect blue walls and stop when confirmed in target zone

The champion uses repulsion in state 1 which seems odd for aggregation... wait, the mission is called AGGREGATION but the score description says robots outside target region. Let me re-read... "score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region." So it's a gathering/herding task to get robots INTO the blue-walled strip.

So repulsion might help spread robots to find the blue strip faster, or help them not cluster in the wrong strip. Let me try more targeted approaches.

## Reasoning

The new champion (18414.39) is a slight improvement. Let me analyze it carefully:

**Champion structure:**
- **S0**: GoToColor(blue=3), LED off
  - →S2 if sees blue (p=0.58)
  - →S1 if black floor (p=0.115)
  - →S2 if gray floor (p=0.17) [D=1 from s0 = state2? Wait: from s0, others=[1,2], D=0→s1, D=1→s2. n0x0 D=1→s2, n0x1 D=0→s1, n0x2 D=1→s2, n0x3 D=1→s2]
  - →S2 if white floor (p=0.60)
- **S1**: Repulsion(4.1601), LED cyan
  - →S2 if neighbors>2 (p=2, w=17.85) [D=1 from s1, others=[0,2], D=1→s2]
  - →S2 if neighbors>5 (p=5, w=12.97) [D=1→s2]
  - →S2 if gray floor (p=0.52) [D=1→s2]
  - →S2 if neighbors<7 (p=7, w=14.59) [D=1→s2]
- **S2**: Exploration(rwm=40), LED off
  - →S2 if gray floor (p=0.32) [D=1 from s2, others=[0,1], D=1→s1... wait: from s2, others=[0,1], D=0→s0, D=1→s1]
  - →S0 if neighbors>7 (p=7, w=10.99) [D=0→s0]

So the flow is:
- S0 (seek blue) → S1 (repulse) when on black floor
- S0 (seek blue) → S2 (explore) when sees blue, gray floor, or white floor
- S1 (repulse) → S2 (explore) on almost any condition
- S2 (explore) → S1 (repulse) on gray floor (p=0.32)
- S2 (explore) → S0 (seek blue) when many neighbors

This is interesting: repulsion happens when on black floor (wrong strip perhaps?), and exploration happens when seeing blue or on gray. The logic seems to be: when on black floor → repulse to spread out, when seeing blue → explore more carefully.

**Key observations:**
1. The champion transitions ALL from S1 go to S2 (explore), making S1 a transient repulsion state
2. S2 transitions: gray floor→S1 (repulse when in middle), many neighbors→S0 (seek blue when clustered)
3. S0 transitions mostly to S2 except black floor→S1

**Exploitation strategy:** Tweak probabilities around the champion, especially:
- The p values in S0 transitions (0.58, 0.115, 0.17, 0.60)
- The rep gain (4.1601)
- The rwm (40)
- The neighbor thresholds in S2

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Huge improvement! Score dropped from 18414 to 13635. The key change in the new champion is **S1 changed from Repulsion to GoAwayColor(green=2)**. This makes perfect sense:

**Champion analysis:**
- **S0**: GoToColor(blue=3) — seeks blue walls
  - →S2 if sees blue (p=0.58)
  - →S1 if black floor (p=0.115)  
  - →S2 if gray floor (p=0.17)
  - →S2 if white floor (p=0.60)
- **S1**: GoAwayColor(green=2) — flees green walls, LED cyan
  - →S2 if neighbors>2 (p=2, w=17.85)
  - →S2 if neighbors>5 (p=5, w=12.97)
  - →S2 if gray floor (p=0.52)
  - →S2 if neighbors<7 (p=7, w=14.59)
- **S2**: Exploration(rwm=40) — random walk
  - →S1 if gray floor (p=0.32)
  - →S0 if neighbors>7 (p=7, w=10.99)

The logic: GoToColor(blue) is the primary driver. When on black floor (could be wrong strip), switch to GoAwayColor(green) to flee the green-walled strip. Exploration helps find blue walls. The combination of seeking blue AND fleeing green is very effective.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tune the probabilities in S0 (0.58, 0.115, 0.17, 0.60)
2. Tune S2 transitions (0.32, threshold 7)
3. Tune S1 transitions (neighbor thresholds)
4. Try different rwm values for S2

**Key insight:** When on black floor, robot might be in either strip. GoAwayColor(green) will push it toward blue. When it sees blue, it switches to exploration (staying near blue). This is elegant.

**Exploration ideas:**
1. Add a 4th state: Stop when both on black floor AND seeing blue (confirmed in target)
2. Try GoToColor(blue) as S1 instead of GoAwayColor(green), or combine both
3. Try S1 transitions that go back to S0 instead of S2
4. Make S1 transitions smarter - go directly to S0 when gray floor

- →S2 if neighbors>5 (w=12.97)
- →S2 if gray floor (p=0.48)
- →S2 if neighbors<7 (w=14.59)

This is very logical: GoAwayColor(green) until you see blue walls, then switch to exploration near blue.

**Full flow:**
- S0 GoToColor(blue): if sees blue→S2(explore), if black floor→S1(flee green), if gray→S2(explore), if white→S2(explore)
- S1 GoAwayColor(green): if sees blue→S2(explore), if many neighbors→S2, if gray→S2, if few neighbors→S2
- S2 Explore(rwm=44): if gray floor→S1(flee green), if many neighbors(>8)→S0(seek blue)

- S0: GoToColor(blue): sees blue→S2(p=0.58), black floor→S1(p=0.115), gray→S2(p=0.17), white→S2(p=0.60)
- S1: GoAwayColor(green): sees blue→S2(p=0.72), neighbors>5→S2, gray→S2(p=0.44), neighbors<7→S2
- S2: Explore(rwm=44): gray→S1(p=0.28), neighbors>8→S0

**Trends to exploit:**
- p(blue) in S1: 0.65→0.72, try 0.78-0.85
- gray p in S1: 0.48→0.44, try 0.38-0.42
- rwm in S2: stable at 44, try 46-50
- gray p in S2: stable at 0.28, try 0.22-0.26
- neighbor threshold in S2: stable at 8, try 7 or 9

**Structural ideas:**
1. What if S1's "sees blue" transition goes to S0 instead of S2? (seek blue more aggressively)
2. Add transition in S2: if sees blue → S0 (seek blue directly)
3. What if we reduce S1 transitions — maybe fewer is better?
4. Try S0 with transition: if sees green → S1 (flee green immediately)

**Important observation:** All S1 transitions go to S2. What if "sees blue" in S1 went to S0 instead? That would make the robot immediately seek blue when it detects it while fleeing green — more direct path to target.

## Reasoning

The champion didn't improve in round 9 (stuck at 12961.47). This is the final round, so I need to maximize the chance of finding a better solution.

**Champion analysis (stable for 2 rounds):**
- S0: GoToColor(blue): sees blue→S2(0.58), black→S1(0.115), gray→S2(0.17), white→S2(0.60)
- S1: GoAwayColor(green): sees blue→S2(0.72), neighbors>5→S2, gray→S2(0.44), neighbors<7→S2
- S2: Explore(rwm=44): gray→S1(0.28), neighbors>7→S0

**What hasn't been tried much:**
1. S1 "sees blue" → S0 directly (not S2) — more aggressive blue seeking
2. S2 with "sees blue" → S0 transition added
3. S0 with "sees green" → S1 transition (immediate flee when green detected)
4. Different S1 neighbor thresholds (3, 4, 6, 8)
5. Very different rwm values (30, 60)
6. S2 gray p much lower (0.15, 0.18) or higher (0.35)
7. Completely different 4-state topology

**This is the last round** — maximize diversity while keeping champion and close variants:
- 1 exact champion copy
- 4 close parameter variants
- 4 structural variants (S1→S0 on blue, extra S2 transition, etc.)
- 3 more exploratory machines

Key untested idea: when in S1 (GoAwayColor green) and sees blue → go to S0 (GoToColor blue) directly rather than S2. This creates a more direct path: flee green until you see blue, then actively chase it.

Another: when in S2 (exploring) and sees blue → go to S0 directly to seek blue more aggressively.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
